========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, Doctor. Well, um, it’s a little hard to know exactly where to start, but I guess what finally made me actually pick up the phone and ask for help is that I just keep forgetting these little things that I normally handle without any issue. 

I work as an administrative assistant, right? So my whole day is basically keeping track of details, and lately, it feels like things are just slipping right through my fingers. Like, just the other day, I was supposed to return a very simple phone call. I even wrote it down, but I completely forgot to actually do it until someone asked me about it later. It’s so embarrassing. And it’s happening with my personal stuff, too. I had this registration form that I needed to drop off—I had already filled the whole thing out, it was sitting right there in my bag, and I just kept forgetting to turn it in. I think I carried it around for a week. 

And then, I don't know, it's just the day-to-day stuff. Like, I'll tell myself I need to stop for a quick errand on the way home from work. I'll be thinking about it when I leave the parking lot, and then somehow I just go on autopilot, and the next thing I know, I'm pulling into my driveway and I realize I drove right past the store. It’s just... it’s all these little things adding up, and it’s driving me crazy because I should be able to just do them, you know?

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, gosh, yes. I mean, it’s actually kind of funny you ask that because looking back, it really has been there the whole time. Even way back in elementary school, I was totally that kid. You know the one—I was always the kid who forgot permission slips for field trips. I would be so excited to go to the zoo or the museum or wherever, and I’d have the paper, but it would just never make it out of my bag and into my teacher's hands, or I'd leave it sitting right on the kitchen counter at home after my mom signed it. 

My backpack was basically a black hole. I would literally do my homework, spend all evening on it, and then somehow just lose it in my backpack. The teacher would ask for it, and I’d be frantically digging around in all these crumpled papers and old wrappers, and it was just gone. It was so frustrating because I actually did the work! 

And big assignments? Oh, those were a nightmare. I could never just sit down and do a diorama or a book report on my own. I always needed my mother, or sometimes my teacher, to sit there and remind me step by step to finish projects. They’d have to be like, "Okay, did you get the shoebox? Okay, good, now did you cut the construction paper? Okay, now glue it." If they didn't hover and walk me through every single little step and keep pulling my attention back, I would just drift off and it would never get done. So, yeah, it’s definitely not a new thing at all, it’s just... I guess back then I had adults managing it for me, and now I'm the adult, you know? And it's just so exhausting having to be the one to constantly monitor myself now that I don't have my mom looking over my shoulder.

No, not really. I mean, I wouldn't say I feel depressed. I definitely get frustrated, you know? Like I was saying, it's just so exhausting trying to keep all these plates spinning, and when one drops, I definitely beat myself up about it. So, yeah, I still have fun and look forward to things. It’s really just the annoyance and the stress of feeling like my brain is a sieve sometimes. But actual depression? No, I don't think so. No, no, nothing like that. I've never hit my head or been in a car accident or had any kind of, you know, brain injury or stroke or anything scary like that. I mean, I guess I probably bumped my head a few times as a kid, like everyone does, right? I remember this one time I was maybe seven or eight, and I was running around the backyard with my cousin, and I tripped over the garden hose and sort of smacked my forehead on the edge of the patio planter. My mom was so worried, she put this bag of frozen peas on it, and I had a pretty good bruise for a week, but I didn't black out or have to go to the hospital or anything. It was just a normal kid bump. 

But as far as an actual medical event that changed my memory? No, definitely not. Like I was saying before, this isn't some sudden new thing that just popped up out of nowhere after an accident. It's really been this continuous thread my whole life, ever since I was little, just losing things and forgetting things. It hasn't suddenly gotten drastically worse because of a head injury or a sickness or anything. It's more that my responsibilities have gotten bigger, so the forgetting is just more noticeable and more of a problem now. But medically, physically, my brain is totally fine, as far as I know! I've always been pretty healthy, honestly. I rarely even get colds. So, no, no head injuries or anything like that at all.

No, not really. I mean, I was never the kid bouncing off the walls or anything like that. I know that's what people usually think of when they think of... well, you know, kids who struggle in school. They picture the little boy who won't stay in his seat and is always running around the classroom and interrupting the teacher. But that was never me. I was actually pretty quiet. I would just sit at my desk, but my mind would be a million miles away. 

I remember my third-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable—she was so sweet, she had this whole collection of ceramic owls on her desk—she used to tell my mom that I was a "daydreamer." I would just sit there staring out the window, watching the clouds or the birds or whatever, and I wouldn't even realize the class had moved on to a completely different subject. But I wasn't running around or fidgeting or anything. I was perfectly content to just sit there, it's just that I wasn't actually paying attention to the math lesson. 

Even at home, I wasn't super hyper. I liked to read, or at least I tried to read, even if I had to re-read the same page three times because I kept zoning out. I liked drawing and doing quiet things. My mom never complained about me being too wild or having too much energy. If anything, she complained that I was in my own little world too much. So, yeah, the physical hyperactivity, the not being able to sit still... that just wasn't me. I've always been more of the "lost in thought" type, not the "can't sit still" type. It’s funny how different it can look, isn't it? Because I definitely struggled, just in a much quieter, less disruptive way.

And it's just so exhausting having to be the one to constantly monitor myself now that I don't have my mom looking over my shoulder.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days lately?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I wouldn't say I feel depressed. I definitely get frustrated, you know? Like I was saying, it's just so exhausting trying to keep all these plates spinning, and when one drops, I definitely beat myself up about it. Like with that phone call I forgot to make, I just sat there at my desk afterward thinking, \"Come on, you're 36 years old, you should be able to remember to make a simple phone call.\" So there are definitely moments where I feel bad about myself, or I get overwhelmed and maybe a little tearful just because it's a lot to manage, but it's not like a deep, dark sadness that lingers all day. \n\nI don't wake up feeling hopeless or anything like that. I actually still enjoy my life, and I love spending time with my friends and my husband. We actually just went to this really great Italian place over the weekend—he surprised me because I'd been having kind of a stressful week with all the stuff at work, and it was so nice to just relax and have a glass of wine. So, yeah, I still have fun and look forward to things. It’s really just the annoyance and the stress of feeling like my brain is a sieve sometimes. But actual depression? No, I don't think so. It's more just... situational frustration, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a head injury or other medical event that seemed to cause these memory problems to start or get much worse?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I've never hit my head or been in a car accident or had any kind of, you know, brain injury or stroke or anything scary like that. I mean, I guess I probably bumped my head a few times as a kid, like everyone does, right? I remember this one time I was maybe seven or eight, and I was running around the backyard with my cousin, and I tripped over the garden hose and sort of smacked my forehead on the edge of the patio planter. My mom was so worried, she put this bag of frozen peas on it, and I had a pretty good bruise for a week, but I didn't black out or have to go to the hospital or anything. It was just a normal kid bump. \n\nBut as far as an actual medical event that changed my memory? No, definitely not. Like I was saying before, this isn't some sudden new thing that just popped up out of nowhere after an accident. It's really been this continuous thread my whole life, ever since I was little, just losing things and forgetting things. It hasn't suddenly gotten drastically worse because of a head injury or a sickness or anything. It's more that my responsibilities have gotten bigger, so the forgetting is just more noticeable and more of a problem now. But medically, physically, my brain is totally fine, as far as I know! I've always been pretty healthy, honestly. I rarely even get colds. So, no, no head injuries or anything like that at all.\n\nDoctor: As a child, were you also often overly active or unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I was never the kid bouncing off the walls or anything like that. I know that's what people usually think of when they think of... well, you know, kids who struggle in school. They picture the little boy who won't stay in his seat and is always running around the classroom and interrupting the teacher. But that was never me. I was actually pretty quiet. I would just sit at my desk, but my mind would be a million miles away. \n\nI remember my third-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable—she was so sweet, she had this whole collection of ceramic owls on her desk—she used to tell my mom that I was a \"daydreamer.\" I would just sit there staring out the window, watching the clouds or the birds or whatever, and I wouldn't even realize the class had moved on to a completely different subject. But I wasn't running around or fidgeting or anything. I was perfectly content to just sit there, it's just that I wasn't actually paying attention to the math lesson. \n\nEven at home, I wasn't super hyper. I liked to read, or at least I tried to read, even if I had to re-read the same page three times because I kept zoning out. I liked drawing and doing quiet things. My mom never complained about me being too wild or having too much energy. If anything, she complained that I was in my own little world too much. So, yeah, the physical hyperactivity, the not being able to sit still... that just wasn't me. I've always been more of the \"lost in thought\" type, not the \"can't sit still\" type. It’s funny how different it can look, isn't it? Because I definitely struggled, just in a much quieter, less disruptive way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I know we kind of touched on this a minute ago, but no, definitely not. I haven't been feeling hopeless or depressed over the last couple of weeks. Like I was saying, I get frustrated, sure. I mean, just the other day I was trying to organize the files for this big meeting we have coming up at work—I'm an administrative assistant, so keeping everything straight is literally my entire job description—and I just felt so overwhelmed because I kept losing my train of thought and forgetting which folders I had already updated. It's incredibly annoying, and I definitely had a moment where I just put my head on my desk and sighed, feeling like I was failing at basic tasks. 

But that's not the same as feeling depressed, right? I don't feel that heavy, dark cloud that people talk about. I don't wake up dreading the day or feeling like there's no point to anything. Even on my worst days, when my brain feels like absolute mush and I've forgotten three different things before lunch, I still look forward to going home and watching my favorite shows, or planning what to make for dinner. In fact, I've been really getting into trying new recipes lately. I found this amazing one for a lemon-garlic chicken dish on Pinterest last week, and it turned out so well! So, yeah, I definitely have moments of feeling down about my memory and my focus, but it's very specific to those struggles. It's not a general feeling of hopelessness or depression at all. I still very much enjoy my life, even when it feels a bit messy.

It’s just... it’s all these little things adding up, and it’s driving me crazy because I should be able to just do them, you know?\n\nDoctor: Did these kinds of forgetfulness or distractibility issues start when you were a child or teenager?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh, yes. I mean, it’s actually kind of funny you ask that because looking back, it really has been there the whole time. Even way back in elementary school, I was totally that kid. You know the one—I was always the kid who forgot permission slips for field trips. I would be so excited to go to the zoo or the museum or wherever, and I’d have the paper, but it would just never make it out of my bag and into my teacher's hands, or I'd leave it sitting right on the kitchen counter at home after my mom signed it. \n\nMy backpack was basically a black hole. I would literally do my homework, spend all evening on it, and then somehow just lose it in my backpack. The teacher would ask for it, and I’d be frantically digging around in all these crumpled papers and old wrappers, and it was just gone. It was so frustrating because I actually did the work! \n\nAnd big assignments? Oh, those were a nightmare. I could never just sit down and do a diorama or a book report on my own. I always needed my mother, or sometimes my teacher, to sit there and remind me step by step to finish projects. They’d have to be like, \"Okay, did you get the shoebox? Okay, good, now did you cut the construction paper? Okay, now glue it.\" If they didn't hover and walk me through every single little step and keep pulling my attention back, I would just drift off and it would never get done. So, yeah, it’s definitely not a new thing at all, it’s just... I guess back then I had adults managing it for me, and now I'm the adult, you know? And it's just so exhausting having to be the one to constantly monitor myself now that I don't have my mom looking over my shoulder.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days lately?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I wouldn't say I feel depressed. I definitely get frustrated, you know? Like I was saying, it's just so exhausting trying to keep all these plates spinning, and when one drops, I definitely beat myself up about it. Like with that phone call I forgot to make, I just sat there at my desk afterward thinking, \"Come on, you're 36 years old, you should be able to remember to make a simple phone call.\" So there are definitely moments where I feel bad about myself, or I get overwhelmed and maybe a little tearful just because it's a lot to manage, but it's not like a deep, dark sadness that lingers all day. \n\nI don't wake up feeling hopeless or anything like that. I actually still enjoy my life, and I love spending time with my friends and my husband. We actually just went to this really great Italian place over the weekend—he surprised me because I'd been having kind of a stressful week with all the stuff at work, and it was so nice to just relax and have a glass of wine. So, yeah, I still have fun and look forward to things. It’s really just the annoyance and the stress of feeling like my brain is a sieve sometimes. But actual depression? No, I don't think so. It's more just... situational frustration, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a head injury or other medical event that seemed to cause these memory problems to start or get much worse?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I've never hit my head or been in a car accident or had any kind of, you know, brain injury or stroke or anything scary like that. I mean, I guess I probably bumped my head a few times as a kid, like everyone does, right? I remember this one time I was maybe seven or eight, and I was running around the backyard with my cousin, and I tripped over the garden hose and sort of smacked my forehead on the edge of the patio planter. My mom was so worried, she put this bag of frozen peas on it, and I had a pretty good bruise for a week, but I didn't black out or have to go to the hospital or anything. It was just a normal kid bump. \n\nBut as far as an actual medical event that changed my memory? No, definitely not. Like I was saying before, this isn't some sudden new thing that just popped up out of nowhere after an accident. It's really been this continuous thread my whole life, ever since I was little, just losing things and forgetting things. It hasn't suddenly gotten drastically worse because of a head injury or a sickness or anything. It's more that my responsibilities have gotten bigger, so the forgetting is just more noticeable and more of a problem now. But medically, physically, my brain is totally fine, as far as I know! I've always been pretty healthy, honestly. I rarely even get colds. So, no, no head injuries or anything like that at all.\n\nDoctor: As a child, were you also often overly active or unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I was never the kid bouncing off the walls or anything like that. I know that's what people usually think of when they think of... well, you know, kids who struggle in school. They picture the little boy who won't stay in his seat and is always running around the classroom and interrupting the teacher. But that was never me. I was actually pretty quiet. I would just sit at my desk, but my mind would be a million miles away. \n\nI remember my third-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable—she was so sweet, she had this whole collection of ceramic owls on her desk—she used to tell my mom that I was a \"daydreamer.\" I would just sit there staring out the window, watching the clouds or the birds or whatever, and I wouldn't even realize the class had moved on to a completely different subject. But I wasn't running around or fidgeting or anything. I was perfectly content to just sit there, it's just that I wasn't actually paying attention to the math lesson. \n\nEven at home, I wasn't super hyper. I liked to read, or at least I tried to read, even if I had to re-read the same page three times because I kept zoning out. I liked drawing and doing quiet things. My mom never complained about me being too wild or having too much energy. If anything, she complained that I was in my own little world too much. So, yeah, the physical hyperactivity, the not being able to sit still... that just wasn't me. I've always been more of the \"lost in thought\" type, not the \"can't sit still\" type. It’s funny how different it can look, isn't it? Because I definitely struggled, just in a much quieter, less disruptive way.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you been feeling down, depressed, or hopeless most days?\n\nPatient: I know we kind of touched on this a minute ago, but no, definitely not. I was perfectly content to just sit there, it's just that I wasn't actually paying attention to the math lesson. \n\nEven at home, I wasn't super hyper. I liked to read, or at least I tried to read, even if I had to re-read the same page three times because I kept zoning out. I liked drawing and doing quiet things. My mom never complained about me being too wild or having too much energy. If anything, she complained that I was in my own little world too much. So, yeah, the physical hyperactivity, the not being able to sit still... that just wasn't me. I've always been more of the \"lost in thought\" type, not the \"can't sit still\" type. It’s funny how different it can look, isn't it? Because I definitely struggled, just in a much quieter, less disruptive way.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you been feeling down, depressed, or hopeless most days?\n\nPatient: I know we kind of touched on this a minute ago, but no, definitely not. I haven't been feeling hopeless or depressed over the last couple of weeks. Like I was saying, I get frustrated, sure. I mean, just the other day I was trying to organize the files for this big meeting we have coming up at work—I'm an administrative assistant, so keeping everything straight is literally my entire job description—and I just felt so overwhelmed because I kept losing my train of thought and forgetting which folders I had already updated. It's incredibly annoying, and I definitely had a moment where I just put my head on my desk and sighed, feeling like I was failing at basic tasks. \n\nBut that's not the same as feeling depressed, right? I don't feel that heavy, dark cloud that people talk about. I don't wake up dreading the day or feeling like there's no point to anything. Even on my worst days, when my brain feels like absolute mush and I've forgotten three different things before lunch, I still look forward to going home and watching my favorite shows, or planning what to make for dinner. In fact, I've been really getting into trying new recipes lately. I found this amazing one for a lemon-garlic chicken dish on Pinterest last week, and it turned out so well! So, yeah, I definitely have moments of feeling down about my memory and my focus, but it's very specific to those struggles. It's not a general feeling of hopelessness or depression at all. I still very much enjoy my life, even when it feels a bit messy.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you also have these same attention and forgetfulness problems outside of work, like at home or in social situations?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hmm... you know, I'm not really sure, to be honest. I haven't really noticed it being the same kind of problem outside of work. I mean, at work, like I was saying, I'm an administrative assistant, so if I forget to reply to an important email or if I misplace a physical file, it's a huge deal. My boss needs things right then and there. But at home... well, the stakes are just so much lower, you know? 

Like, if I'm at my apartment—I have this cute little one-bedroom over on 4th Street, it's got these great big windows in the living room, which I absolutely love because I have a ton of houseplants. I'm practically running a jungle in there! I have pothos, snake plants, and this fiddle leaf fig that is being very temperamental right now, dropping leaves if I even look at it wrong. But anyway, my point is, if I intend to water them on a Tuesday and I completely forget and don't remember until Thursday, it doesn't really matter. The plants don't fire me. Or if I start doing laundry and then wander off to make a cup of tea and completely forget about the clothes sitting in the washer until the next morning... I just run the rinse cycle again. It's annoying, sure, but it's not a crisis. So I guess I haven't really noticed if I'm exactly as forgetful at home, because it just doesn't cause the same kind of panic or get me in trouble. 

And socially... I don't know, I'm really not sure if it affects me there either. My friends and I, we usually just do super low-key stuff. Like last weekend, my friend Sarah came over—we've known each other since high school, actually, we were in the exact same homeroom all four years—and we just ordered Thai food and watched a movie. I got the Pad Kee Mao, which was incredibly spicy, way spicier than they usually make it! But anyway, I think I might zone out a little bit sometimes when we're hanging out, or maybe I'll forget a story she told me a few weeks ago, but she knows me so well that she just repeats it and we just kind of laugh about it. It's not like a strict environment where I'm being graded on my attention span or my memory. 

So, yeah, I really haven't noticed it being a big, glaring issue in my personal life. Or at least, I'm not sure if it's the exact same problem, because my personal life is just so much more relaxed and forgiving than my job. Does that make sense? Because I just don't have those rigid deadlines or people waiting on me at home, so if my brain wanders off, it's just... fine.

I mean, I wouldn't say I feel depressed. I definitely get frustrated, you know? Like I was saying, it's just so exhausting trying to keep all these plates spinning, and when one drops, I definitely beat myself up about it. Like with that phone call I forgot to make, I just sat there at my desk afterward thinking, \"Come on, you're 36 years old, you should be able to remember to make a simple phone call.\" So there are definitely moments where I feel bad about myself, or I get overwhelmed and maybe a little tearful just because it's a lot to manage, but it's not like a deep, dark sadness that lingers all day. \n\nI don't wake up feeling hopeless or anything like that. I actually still enjoy my life, and I love spending time with my friends and my husband. We actually just went to this really great Italian place over the weekend—he surprised me because I'd been having kind of a stressful week with all the stuff at work, and it was so nice to just relax and have a glass of wine. So, yeah, I still have fun and look forward to things. It’s really just the annoyance and the stress of feeling like my brain is a sieve sometimes. But actual depression? No, I don't think so. It's more just... situational frustration, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a head injury or other medical event that seemed to cause these memory problems to start or get much worse?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I've never hit my head or been in a car accident or had any kind of, you know, brain injury or stroke or anything scary like that. I mean, I guess I probably bumped my head a few times as a kid, like everyone does, right? I remember this one time I was maybe seven or eight, and I was running around the backyard with my cousin, and I tripped over the garden hose and sort of smacked my forehead on the edge of the patio planter. My mom was so worried, she put this bag of frozen peas on it, and I had a pretty good bruise for a week, but I didn't black out or have to go to the hospital or anything. It was just a normal kid bump. \n\nBut as far as an actual medical event that changed my memory? No, definitely not. Like I was saying before, this isn't some sudden new thing that just popped up out of nowhere after an accident. It's really been this continuous thread my whole life, ever since I was little, just losing things and forgetting things. It hasn't suddenly gotten drastically worse because of a head injury or a sickness or anything. It's more that my responsibilities have gotten bigger, so the forgetting is just more noticeable and more of a problem now. But medically, physically, my brain is totally fine, as far as I know! I've always been pretty healthy, honestly. I rarely even get colds. So, no, no head injuries or anything like that at all.\n\nDoctor: As a child, were you also often overly active or unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I was never the kid bouncing off the walls or anything like that. I know that's what people usually think of when they think of... well, you know, kids who struggle in school. They picture the little boy who won't stay in his seat and is always running around the classroom and interrupting the teacher. But that was never me. I was actually pretty quiet. I would just sit at my desk, but my mind would be a million miles away. \n\nI remember my third-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable—she was so sweet, she had this whole collection of ceramic owls on her desk—she used to tell my mom that I was a \"daydreamer.\" I would just sit there staring out the window, watching the clouds or the birds or whatever, and I wouldn't even realize the class had moved on to a completely different subject. But I wasn't running around or fidgeting or anything. I was perfectly content to just sit there, it's just that I wasn't actually paying attention to the math lesson. \n\nEven at home, I wasn't super hyper. I liked to read, or at least I tried to read, even if I had to re-read the same page three times because I kept zoning out. I liked drawing and doing quiet things. My mom never complained about me being too wild or having too much energy. If anything, she complained that I was in my own little world too much. So, yeah, the physical hyperactivity, the not being able to sit still... that just wasn't me. I've always been more of the \"lost in thought\" type, not the \"can't sit still\" type. It’s funny how different it can look, isn't it? Because I definitely struggled, just in a much quieter, less disruptive way.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you been feeling down, depressed, or hopeless most days?\n\nPatient: I know we kind of touched on this a minute ago, but no, definitely not. I haven't been feeling hopeless or depressed over the last couple of weeks. Like I was saying, I get frustrated, sure. I mean, just the other day I was trying to organize the files for this big meeting we have coming up at work—I'm an administrative assistant, so keeping everything straight is literally my entire job description—and I just felt so overwhelmed because I kept losing my train of thought and forgetting which folders I had already updated. It's incredibly annoying, and I definitely had a moment where I just put my head on my desk and sighed, feeling like I was failing at basic tasks. \n\nBut that's not the same as feeling depressed, right? I don't feel that heavy, dark cloud that people talk about. I don't wake up dreading the day or feeling like there's no point to anything. Even on my worst days, when my brain feels like absolute mush and I've forgotten three different things before lunch, I still look forward to going home and watching my favorite shows, or planning what to make for dinner. In fact, I've been really getting into trying new recipes lately. I found this amazing one for a lemon-garlic chicken dish on Pinterest last week, and it turned out so well! So, yeah, I definitely have moments of feeling down about my memory and my focus, but it's very specific to those struggles. It's not a general feeling of hopelessness or depression at all. I still very much enjoy my life, even when it feels a bit messy.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have these same attention and forgetfulness problems outside of work, like at home or in social situations?\n\nPatient: Hmm... you know, I'm not really sure, to be honest. I haven't really noticed it being the same kind of problem outside of work. I mean, at work, like I was saying, I'm an administrative assistant, so if I forget to reply to an important email or if I misplace a physical file, it's a huge deal. My boss needs things right then and there. But at home... well, the stakes are just so much lower, you know? \n\nLike, if I'm at my apartment—I have this cute little one-bedroom over on 4th Street, it's got these great big windows in the living room, which I absolutely love because I have a ton of houseplants. I'm practically running a jungle in there! I have pothos, snake plants, and this fiddle leaf fig that is being very temperamental right now, dropping leaves if I even look at it wrong. But anyway, my point is, if I intend to water them on a Tuesday and I completely forget and don't remember until Thursday, it doesn't really matter. The plants don't fire me. Or if I start doing laundry and then wander off to make a cup of tea and completely forget about the clothes sitting in the washer until the next morning... I just run the rinse cycle again. It's annoying, sure, but it's not a crisis. So I guess I haven't really noticed if I'm exactly as forgetful at home, because it just doesn't cause the same kind of panic or get me in trouble. \n\nAnd socially... I don't know, I'm really not sure if it affects me there either. My friends and I, we usually just do super low-key stuff. Like last weekend, my friend Sarah came over—we've known each other since high school, actually, we were in the exact same homeroom all four years—and we just ordered Thai food and watched a movie. I got the Pad Kee Mao, which was incredibly spicy, way spicier than they usually make it! But anyway, I think I might zone out a little bit sometimes when we're hanging out, or maybe I'll forget a story she told me a few weeks ago, but she knows me so well that she just repeats it and we just kind of laugh about it. It's not like a strict environment where I'm being graded on my attention span or my memory. \n\nSo, yeah, I really haven't noticed it being a big, glaring issue in my personal life. Or at least, I'm not sure if it's the exact same problem, because my personal life is just so much more relaxed and forgiving than my job. Does that make sense? Because I just don't have those rigid deadlines or people waiting on me at home, so if my brain wanders off, it's just... fine.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, like I was just saying about my apartment and my friends, home is pretty forgiving, so I don't really notice it as a crisis there. But if you mean, is it happening in more than one area where it actually has real consequences... then yes, definitely. Because it's not just my daily job duties, it's my classes, too. 

I don't think I mentioned this yet, but I'm actually taking a couple of evening and online classes at the community college—just some business administration and accounting courses, trying to better myself, you know? And I mean, I’m still getting by overall. I haven't failed out, and I haven't been fired. But I’ve missed small work follow-ups, like forgetting to confirm a catering order for a meeting until the absolute last minute, or not replying to a vendor because I opened the email, got distracted by a phone call, and then it just got buried in my inbox. 

And then with school, it's the same exact thing. I've been missing school deadlines left and right lately. I'll read the syllabus, tell myself a paper is due on Thursday, and then suddenly it's Friday morning and I realize I completely forgot to submit it, or I'll miss those weekly discussion board posts entirely. It's incredibly frustrating. 

So between work and school, it's happening often enough that I really worry it could affect my reputation. I don't want people to think I'm flaky or unreliable, because I'm really not, I just... I just lose track of things. And there’s an Office Manager role opening up next year when our current manager, Susan, retires. Susan has been there for twenty years and she is incredibly organized, like, she literally color-codes her highlighters and has a binder for everything. I really, really want that job. I know I could be good at it if I could just get my brain to cooperate. But if my boss sees me dropping the ball on these little follow-ups, or if I can't even keep my school deadlines straight to finish my coursework, I know it's going to ruin my chance at moving into that Office Manager role. So, yeah, it's definitely impacting me in both of those settings, and it just makes me so anxious that I'm going to sabotage my own future without even meaning to.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F41.9, F06.7, F32.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m Dr. ____. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi, Doctor. Well, um, it’s a little hard to know exactly where to start, but I guess what finally made me actually pick up the phone and ask for help is that I just keep forgetting these little things that I normally handle without any issue. \n\nI work as an administrative assistant, right? So my whole day is basically keeping track of details, and lately, it feels like things are just slipping right through my fingers. Like, just the other day, I was supposed to return a very simple phone call. I even wrote it down, but I completely forgot to actually do it until someone asked me about it later. It’s so embarrassing. And it’s happening with my personal stuff, too. I had this registration form that I needed to drop off—I had already filled the whole thing out, it was sitting right there in my bag, and I just kept forgetting to turn it in. I think I carried it around for a week. \n\nAnd then, I don't know, it's just the day-to-day stuff. Like, I'll tell myself I need to stop for a quick errand on the way home from work. I'll be thinking about it when I leave the parking lot, and then somehow I just go on autopilot, and the next thing I know, I'm pulling into my driveway and I realize I drove right past the store. It’s just... it’s all these little things adding up, and it’s driving me crazy because I should be able to just do them, you know?\n\nDoctor: Did these kinds of forgetfulness or distractibility issues start when you were a child or teenager?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh, yes. I mean, it’s actually kind of funny you ask that because looking back, it really has been there the whole time. Even way back in elementary school, I was totally that kid. You know the one—I was always the kid who forgot permission slips for field trips. I would be so excited to go to the zoo or the museum or wherever, and I’d have the paper, but it would just never make it out of my bag and into my teacher's hands, or I'd leave it sitting right on the kitchen counter at home after my mom signed it. \n\nMy backpack was basically a black hole. I would literally do my homework, spend all evening on it, and then somehow just lose it in my backpack. The teacher would ask for it, and I’d be frantically digging around in all these crumpled papers and old wrappers, and it was just gone. It was so frustrating because I actually did the work! \n\nAnd big assignments? Oh, those were a nightmare. I could never just sit down and do a diorama or a book report on my own. I always needed my mother, or sometimes my teacher, to sit there and remind me step by step to finish projects. They’d have to be like, \"Okay, did you get the shoebox? Okay, good, now did you cut the construction paper? Okay, now glue it.\" If they didn't hover and walk me through every single little step and keep pulling my attention back, I would just drift off and it would never get done. So, yeah, it’s definitely not a new thing at all, it’s just... I guess back then I had adults managing it for me, and now I'm the adult, you know? And it's just so exhausting having to be the one to constantly monitor myself now that I don't have my mom looking over my shoulder.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days lately?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I wouldn't say I feel depressed. I definitely get frustrated, you know? Like I was saying, it's just so exhausting trying to keep all these plates spinning, and when one drops, I definitely beat myself up about it. Like with that phone call I forgot to make, I just sat there at my desk afterward thinking, \"Come on, you're 36 years old, you should be able to remember to make a simple phone call.\" So there are definitely moments where I feel bad about myself, or I get overwhelmed and maybe a little tearful just because it's a lot to manage, but it's not like a deep, dark sadness that lingers all day. \n\nI don't wake up feeling hopeless or anything like that. I actually still enjoy my life, and I love spending time with my friends and my husband. We actually just went to this really great Italian place over the weekend—he surprised me because I'd been having kind of a stressful week with all the stuff at work, and it was so nice to just relax and have a glass of wine. So, yeah, I still have fun and look forward to things. It’s really just the annoyance and the stress of feeling like my brain is a sieve sometimes. But actual depression? No, I don't think so. It's more just... situational frustration, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a head injury or other medical event that seemed to cause these memory problems to start or get much worse?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I've never hit my head or been in a car accident or had any kind of, you know, brain injury or stroke or anything scary like that. I mean, I guess I probably bumped my head a few times as a kid, like everyone does, right? I remember this one time I was maybe seven or eight, and I was running around the backyard with my cousin, and I tripped over the garden hose and sort of smacked my forehead on the edge of the patio planter. My mom was so worried, she put this bag of frozen peas on it, and I had a pretty good bruise for a week, but I didn't black out or have to go to the hospital or anything. It was just a normal kid bump. \n\nBut as far as an actual medical event that changed my memory? No, definitely not. Like I was saying before, this isn't some sudden new thing that just popped up out of nowhere after an accident. It's really been this continuous thread my whole life, ever since I was little, just losing things and forgetting things. It hasn't suddenly gotten drastically worse because of a head injury or a sickness or anything. It's more that my responsibilities have gotten bigger, so the forgetting is just more noticeable and more of a problem now. But medically, physically, my brain is totally fine, as far as I know! I've always been pretty healthy, honestly. I rarely even get colds. So, no, no head injuries or anything like that at all.\n\nDoctor: As a child, were you also often overly active or unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I was never the kid bouncing off the walls or anything like that. I know that's what people usually think of when they think of... well, you know, kids who struggle in school. They picture the little boy who won't stay in his seat and is always running around the classroom and interrupting the teacher. But that was never me. I was actually pretty quiet. I would just sit at my desk, but my mind would be a million miles away. \n\nI remember my third-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable—she was so sweet, she had this whole collection of ceramic owls on her desk—she used to tell my mom that I was a \"daydreamer.\" I would just sit there staring out the window, watching the clouds or the birds or whatever, and I wouldn't even realize the class had moved on to a completely different subject. But I wasn't running around or fidgeting or anything. I was perfectly content to just sit there, it's just that I wasn't actually paying attention to the math lesson. \n\nEven at home, I wasn't super hyper. I liked to read, or at least I tried to read, even if I had to re-read the same page three times because I kept zoning out. I liked drawing and doing quiet things. My mom never complained about me being too wild or having too much energy. If anything, she complained that I was in my own little world too much. So, yeah, the physical hyperactivity, the not being able to sit still... that just wasn't me. I've always been more of the \"lost in thought\" type, not the \"can't sit still\" type. It’s funny how different it can look, isn't it? Because I definitely struggled, just in a much quieter, less disruptive way.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you been feeling down, depressed, or hopeless most days?\n\nPatient: I know we kind of touched on this a minute ago, but no, definitely not. I haven't been feeling hopeless or depressed over the last couple of weeks. Like I was saying, I get frustrated, sure. I mean, just the other day I was trying to organize the files for this big meeting we have coming up at work—I'm an administrative assistant, so keeping everything straight is literally my entire job description—and I just felt so overwhelmed because I kept losing my train of thought and forgetting which folders I had already updated. It's incredibly annoying, and I definitely had a moment where I just put my head on my desk and sighed, feeling like I was failing at basic tasks. \n\nBut that's not the same as feeling depressed, right? I don't feel that heavy, dark cloud that people talk about. I don't wake up dreading the day or feeling like there's no point to anything. Even on my worst days, when my brain feels like absolute mush and I've forgotten three different things before lunch, I still look forward to going home and watching my favorite shows, or planning what to make for dinner. In fact, I've been really getting into trying new recipes lately. I found this amazing one for a lemon-garlic chicken dish on Pinterest last week, and it turned out so well! So, yeah, I definitely have moments of feeling down about my memory and my focus, but it's very specific to those struggles. It's not a general feeling of hopelessness or depression at all. I still very much enjoy my life, even when it feels a bit messy.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have these same attention and forgetfulness problems outside of work, like at home or in social situations?\n\nPatient: Hmm... you know, I'm not really sure, to be honest. I haven't really noticed it being the same kind of problem outside of work. I mean, at work, like I was saying, I'm an administrative assistant, so if I forget to reply to an important email or if I misplace a physical file, it's a huge deal. My boss needs things right then and there. But at home... well, the stakes are just so much lower, you know? \n\nLike, if I'm at my apartment—I have this cute little one-bedroom over on 4th Street, it's got these great big windows in the living room, which I absolutely love because I have a ton of houseplants. I'm practically running a jungle in there! I have pothos, snake plants, and this fiddle leaf fig that is being very temperamental right now, dropping leaves if I even look at it wrong. But anyway, my point is, if I intend to water them on a Tuesday and I completely forget and don't remember until Thursday, it doesn't really matter. The plants don't fire me. Or if I start doing laundry and then wander off to make a cup of tea and completely forget about the clothes sitting in the washer until the next morning... I just run the rinse cycle again. It's annoying, sure, but it's not a crisis. So I guess I haven't really noticed if I'm exactly as forgetful at home, because it just doesn't cause the same kind of panic or get me in trouble. \n\nAnd socially... I don't know, I'm really not sure if it affects me there either. My friends and I, we usually just do super low-key stuff. Like last weekend, my friend Sarah came over—we've known each other since high school, actually, we were in the exact same homeroom all four years—and we just ordered Thai food and watched a movie. I got the Pad Kee Mao, which was incredibly spicy, way spicier than they usually make it! But anyway, I think I might zone out a little bit sometimes when we're hanging out, or maybe I'll forget a story she told me a few weeks ago, but she knows me so well that she just repeats it and we just kind of laugh about it. It's not like a strict environment where I'm being graded on my attention span or my memory. \n\nSo, yeah, I really haven't noticed it being a big, glaring issue in my personal life. Or at least, I'm not sure if it's the exact same problem, because my personal life is just so much more relaxed and forgiving than my job. Does that make sense? Because I just don't have those rigid deadlines or people waiting on me at home, so if my brain wanders off, it's just... fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice these attention and forgetfulness problems in more than one setting, like at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying about my apartment and my friends, home is pretty forgiving, so I don't really notice it as a crisis there. But if you mean, is it happening in more than one area where it actually has real consequences... then yes, definitely. Because it's not just my daily job duties, it's my classes, too. \n\nI don't think I mentioned this yet, but I'm actually taking a couple of evening and online classes at the community college—just some business administration and accounting courses, trying to better myself, you know? And I mean, I’m still getting by overall. I haven't failed out, and I haven't been fired. But I’ve missed small work follow-ups, like forgetting to confirm a catering order for a meeting until the absolute last minute, or not replying to a vendor because I opened the email, got distracted by a phone call, and then it just got buried in my inbox. \n\nAnd then with school, it's the same exact thing. I've been missing school deadlines left and right lately. I'll read the syllabus, tell myself a paper is due on Thursday, and then suddenly it's Friday morning and I realize I completely forgot to submit it, or I'll miss those weekly discussion board posts entirely. It's incredibly frustrating. \n\nSo between work and school, it's happening often enough that I really worry it could affect my reputation. I don't want people to think I'm flaky or unreliable, because I'm really not, I just... I just lose track of things. And there’s an Office Manager role opening up next year when our current manager, Susan, retires. Susan has been there for twenty years and she is incredibly organized, like, she literally color-codes her highlighters and has a binder for everything. I really, really want that job. I know I could be good at it if I could just get my brain to cooperate. But if my boss sees me dropping the ball on these little follow-ups, or if I can't even keep my school deadlines straight to finish my coursework, I know it's going to ruin my chance at moving into that Office Manager role. So, yeah, it's definitely impacting me in both of those settings, and it just makes me so anxious that I'm going to sabotage my own future without even meaning to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

No, not really. I mean, I don't get that feeling at all, actually. Sitting still is probably the easiest part of my day! You know, we have these really long staff meetings every other Tuesday—well, sometimes they push them to Wednesday if the VP of Sales is flying in, but usually Tuesday mornings—and I’m the one who has to take all the meeting minutes. So I am literally glued to my chair for sometimes two or two and a half hours straight. And physically, I’m totally fine. I don't feel like I need to jump up or pace around or anything like that. We did just get these new ergonomic mesh chairs in the conference room last month, which are honestly a huge upgrade from the old fake leather ones that used to make your back sweat, so maybe that helps. But even before that, I never really had an issue with needing to move.

If anything, my body is perfectly happy just staying put. It’s my *brain* that gets up and walks out of the room, you know? Like, I’ll be sitting there, completely still, looking right at whoever is presenting the Q3 projections, and suddenly I realize I haven't heard a single word they said for the last ten minutes because I was mentally reorganizing my kitchen pantry. But physically? No, I'm not restless at all. 

And it's the exact same thing when it comes to relaxing at home. I have absolutely no problem just melting into my couch. I bought this incredibly soft, chunky knit blanket from Target a few weeks ago—it’s that mustard yellow color that's super trendy right now, it matches my throw pillows perfectly—and once I’m under that with a cup of chamomile tea, I can sit there and binge-watch three or four episodes of whatever true crime documentary I’m into without moving a muscle. I can be a total couch potato. So, yeah, feeling restless or needing to move around really isn't something I struggle with. I can sit still for ages. It's just paying attention while I'm sitting there that's the tricky part!

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F41.9, F06.7, F32.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m Dr. ____. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi, Doctor. Well, um, it’s a little hard to know exactly where to start, but I guess what finally made me actually pick up the phone and ask for help is that I just keep forgetting these little things that I normally handle without any issue. \n\nI work as an administrative assistant, right? So my whole day is basically keeping track of details, and lately, it feels like things are just slipping right through my fingers. Like, just the other day, I was supposed to return a very simple phone call. I even wrote it down, but I completely forgot to actually do it until someone asked me about it later. It’s so embarrassing. And it’s happening with my personal stuff, too. I had this registration form that I needed to drop off—I had already filled the whole thing out, it was sitting right there in my bag, and I just kept forgetting to turn it in. I think I carried it around for a week. \n\nAnd then, I don't know, it's just the day-to-day stuff. Like, I'll tell myself I need to stop for a quick errand on the way home from work. I'll be thinking about it when I leave the parking lot, and then somehow I just go on autopilot, and the next thing I know, I'm pulling into my driveway and I realize I drove right past the store. It’s just... it’s all these little things adding up, and it’s driving me crazy because I should be able to just do them, you know?\n\nDoctor: Did these kinds of forgetfulness or distractibility issues start when you were a child or teenager?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh, yes. I mean, it’s actually kind of funny you ask that because looking back, it really has been there the whole time. Even way back in elementary school, I was totally that kid. You know the one—I was always the kid who forgot permission slips for field trips. I would be so excited to go to the zoo or the museum or wherever, and I’d have the paper, but it would just never make it out of my bag and into my teacher's hands, or I'd leave it sitting right on the kitchen counter at home after my mom signed it. \n\nMy backpack was basically a black hole. I would literally do my homework, spend all evening on it, and then somehow just lose it in my backpack. The teacher would ask for it, and I’d be frantically digging around in all these crumpled papers and old wrappers, and it was just gone. It was so frustrating because I actually did the work! \n\nAnd big assignments? Oh, those were a nightmare. I could never just sit down and do a diorama or a book report on my own. I always needed my mother, or sometimes my teacher, to sit there and remind me step by step to finish projects. They’d have to be like, \"Okay, did you get the shoebox? Okay, good, now did you cut the construction paper? Okay, now glue it.\" If they didn't hover and walk me through every single little step and keep pulling my attention back, I would just drift off and it would never get done. So, yeah, it’s definitely not a new thing at all, it’s just... I guess back then I had adults managing it for me, and now I'm the adult, you know? And it's just so exhausting having to be the one to constantly monitor myself now that I don't have my mom looking over my shoulder.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days lately?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I wouldn't say I feel depressed. I definitely get frustrated, you know? Like I was saying, it's just so exhausting trying to keep all these plates spinning, and when one drops, I definitely beat myself up about it. Like with that phone call I forgot to make, I just sat there at my desk afterward thinking, \"Come on, you're 36 years old, you should be able to remember to make a simple phone call.\" So there are definitely moments where I feel bad about myself, or I get overwhelmed and maybe a little tearful just because it's a lot to manage, but it's not like a deep, dark sadness that lingers all day. \n\nI don't wake up feeling hopeless or anything like that. I actually still enjoy my life, and I love spending time with my friends and my husband. We actually just went to this really great Italian place over the weekend—he surprised me because I'd been having kind of a stressful week with all the stuff at work, and it was so nice to just relax and have a glass of wine. So, yeah, I still have fun and look forward to things. It’s really just the annoyance and the stress of feeling like my brain is a sieve sometimes. But actual depression? No, I don't think so. It's more just... situational frustration, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a head injury or other medical event that seemed to cause these memory problems to start or get much worse?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I've never hit my head or been in a car accident or had any kind of, you know, brain injury or stroke or anything scary like that. I mean, I guess I probably bumped my head a few times as a kid, like everyone does, right? I remember this one time I was maybe seven or eight, and I was running around the backyard with my cousin, and I tripped over the garden hose and sort of smacked my forehead on the edge of the patio planter. My mom was so worried, she put this bag of frozen peas on it, and I had a pretty good bruise for a week, but I didn't black out or have to go to the hospital or anything. It was just a normal kid bump. \n\nBut as far as an actual medical event that changed my memory? No, definitely not. Like I was saying before, this isn't some sudden new thing that just popped up out of nowhere after an accident. It's really been this continuous thread my whole life, ever since I was little, just losing things and forgetting things. It hasn't suddenly gotten drastically worse because of a head injury or a sickness or anything. It's more that my responsibilities have gotten bigger, so the forgetting is just more noticeable and more of a problem now. But medically, physically, my brain is totally fine, as far as I know! I've always been pretty healthy, honestly. I rarely even get colds. So, no, no head injuries or anything like that at all.\n\nDoctor: As a child, were you also often overly active or unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I was never the kid bouncing off the walls or anything like that. I know that's what people usually think of when they think of... well, you know, kids who struggle in school. They picture the little boy who won't stay in his seat and is always running around the classroom and interrupting the teacher. But that was never me. I was actually pretty quiet. I would just sit at my desk, but my mind would be a million miles away. \n\nI remember my third-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable—she was so sweet, she had this whole collection of ceramic owls on her desk—she used to tell my mom that I was a \"daydreamer.\" I would just sit there staring out the window, watching the clouds or the birds or whatever, and I wouldn't even realize the class had moved on to a completely different subject. But I wasn't running around or fidgeting or anything. I was perfectly content to just sit there, it's just that I wasn't actually paying attention to the math lesson. \n\nEven at home, I wasn't super hyper. I liked to read, or at least I tried to read, even if I had to re-read the same page three times because I kept zoning out. I liked drawing and doing quiet things. My mom never complained about me being too wild or having too much energy. If anything, she complained that I was in my own little world too much. So, yeah, the physical hyperactivity, the not being able to sit still... that just wasn't me. I've always been more of the \"lost in thought\" type, not the \"can't sit still\" type. It’s funny how different it can look, isn't it? Because I definitely struggled, just in a much quieter, less disruptive way.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you been feeling down, depressed, or hopeless most days?\n\nPatient: I know we kind of touched on this a minute ago, but no, definitely not. I haven't been feeling hopeless or depressed over the last couple of weeks. Like I was saying, I get frustrated, sure. I mean, just the other day I was trying to organize the files for this big meeting we have coming up at work—I'm an administrative assistant, so keeping everything straight is literally my entire job description—and I just felt so overwhelmed because I kept losing my train of thought and forgetting which folders I had already updated. It's incredibly annoying, and I definitely had a moment where I just put my head on my desk and sighed, feeling like I was failing at basic tasks. \n\nBut that's not the same as feeling depressed, right? I don't feel that heavy, dark cloud that people talk about. I don't wake up dreading the day or feeling like there's no point to anything. Even on my worst days, when my brain feels like absolute mush and I've forgotten three different things before lunch, I still look forward to going home and watching my favorite shows, or planning what to make for dinner. In fact, I've been really getting into trying new recipes lately. I found this amazing one for a lemon-garlic chicken dish on Pinterest last week, and it turned out so well! So, yeah, I definitely have moments of feeling down about my memory and my focus, but it's very specific to those struggles. It's not a general feeling of hopelessness or depression at all. I still very much enjoy my life, even when it feels a bit messy.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have these same attention and forgetfulness problems outside of work, like at home or in social situations?\n\nPatient: Hmm... you know, I'm not really sure, to be honest. I haven't really noticed it being the same kind of problem outside of work. I mean, at work, like I was saying, I'm an administrative assistant, so if I forget to reply to an important email or if I misplace a physical file, it's a huge deal. My boss needs things right then and there. But at home... well, the stakes are just so much lower, you know? \n\nLike, if I'm at my apartment—I have this cute little one-bedroom over on 4th Street, it's got these great big windows in the living room, which I absolutely love because I have a ton of houseplants. I'm practically running a jungle in there! I have pothos, snake plants, and this fiddle leaf fig that is being very temperamental right now, dropping leaves if I even look at it wrong. But anyway, my point is, if I intend to water them on a Tuesday and I completely forget and don't remember until Thursday, it doesn't really matter. The plants don't fire me. Or if I start doing laundry and then wander off to make a cup of tea and completely forget about the clothes sitting in the washer until the next morning... I just run the rinse cycle again. It's annoying, sure, but it's not a crisis. So I guess I haven't really noticed if I'm exactly as forgetful at home, because it just doesn't cause the same kind of panic or get me in trouble. \n\nAnd socially... I don't know, I'm really not sure if it affects me there either. My friends and I, we usually just do super low-key stuff. Like last weekend, my friend Sarah came over—we've known each other since high school, actually, we were in the exact same homeroom all four years—and we just ordered Thai food and watched a movie. I got the Pad Kee Mao, which was incredibly spicy, way spicier than they usually make it! But anyway, I think I might zone out a little bit sometimes when we're hanging out, or maybe I'll forget a story she told me a few weeks ago, but she knows me so well that she just repeats it and we just kind of laugh about it. It's not like a strict environment where I'm being graded on my attention span or my memory. \n\nSo, yeah, I really haven't noticed it being a big, glaring issue in my personal life. Or at least, I'm not sure if it's the exact same problem, because my personal life is just so much more relaxed and forgiving than my job. Does that make sense? Because I just don't have those rigid deadlines or people waiting on me at home, so if my brain wanders off, it's just... fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice these attention and forgetfulness problems in more than one setting, like at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying about my apartment and my friends, home is pretty forgiving, so I don't really notice it as a crisis there. But if you mean, is it happening in more than one area where it actually has real consequences... then yes, definitely. Because it's not just my daily job duties, it's my classes, too. \n\nI don't think I mentioned this yet, but I'm actually taking a couple of evening and online classes at the community college—just some business administration and accounting courses, trying to better myself, you know? And I mean, I’m still getting by overall. I haven't failed out, and I haven't been fired. But I’ve missed small work follow-ups, like forgetting to confirm a catering order for a meeting until the absolute last minute, or not replying to a vendor because I opened the email, got distracted by a phone call, and then it just got buried in my inbox. \n\nAnd then with school, it's the same exact thing. I've been missing school deadlines left and right lately. I'll read the syllabus, tell myself a paper is due on Thursday, and then suddenly it's Friday morning and I realize I completely forgot to submit it, or I'll miss those weekly discussion board posts entirely. It's incredibly frustrating. \n\nSo between work and school, it's happening often enough that I really worry it could affect my reputation. I don't want people to think I'm flaky or unreliable, because I'm really not, I just... I just lose track of things. Susan has been there for twenty years and she is incredibly organized, like, she literally color-codes her highlighters and has a binder for everything. I really, really want that job. I know I could be good at it if I could just get my brain to cooperate. But if my boss sees me dropping the ball on these little follow-ups, or if I can't even keep my school deadlines straight to finish my coursework, I know it's going to ruin my chance at moving into that Office Manager role. So, yeah, it's definitely impacting me in both of those settings, and it just makes me so anxious that I'm going to sabotage my own future without even meaning to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless, like it’s hard to relax or sit through a long meeting without needing to move around?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I don't get that feeling at all, actually. Sitting still is probably the easiest part of my day! You know, we have these really long staff meetings every other Tuesday—well, sometimes they push them to Wednesday if the VP of Sales is flying in, but usually Tuesday mornings—and I’m the one who has to take all the meeting minutes. So I am literally glued to my chair for sometimes two or two and a half hours straight. And physically, I’m totally fine. I don't feel like I need to jump up or pace around or anything like that. Like, I’ll be sitting there, completely still, looking right at whoever is presenting the Q3 projections, and suddenly I realize I haven't heard a single word they said for the last ten minutes because I was mentally reorganizing my kitchen pantry. Or if I start doing laundry and then wander off to make a cup of tea and completely forget about the clothes sitting in the washer until the next morning... I just run the rinse cycle again. It's annoying, sure, but it's not a crisis. So I guess I haven't really noticed if I'm exactly as forgetful at home, because it just doesn't cause the same kind of panic or get me in trouble. \n\nAnd socially... I don't know, I'm really not sure if it affects me there either. My friends and I, we usually just do super low-key stuff. Like last weekend, my friend Sarah came over—we've known each other since high school, actually, we were in the exact same homeroom all four years—and we just ordered Thai food and watched a movie. I got the Pad Kee Mao, which was incredibly spicy, way spicier than they usually make it! But anyway, I think I might zone out a little bit sometimes when we're hanging out, or maybe I'll forget a story she told me a few weeks ago, but she knows me so well that she just repeats it and we just kind of laugh about it. It's not like a strict environment where I'm being graded on my attention span or my memory. \n\nSo, yeah, I really haven't noticed it being a big, glaring issue in my personal life. Or at least, I'm not sure if it's the exact same problem, because my personal life is just so much more relaxed and forgiving than my job. Does that make sense? Because I just don't have those rigid deadlines or people waiting on me at home, so if my brain wanders off, it's just... fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice these attention and forgetfulness problems in more than one setting, like at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying about my apartment and my friends, home is pretty forgiving, so I don't really notice it as a crisis there. But if you mean, is it happening in more than one area where it actually has real consequences... then yes, definitely. Because it's not just my daily job duties, it's my classes, too. \n\nI don't think I mentioned this yet, but I'm actually taking a couple of evening and online classes at the community college—just some business administration and accounting courses, trying to better myself, you know? And I mean, I’m still getting by overall. I haven't failed out, and I haven't been fired. But I’ve missed small work follow-ups, like forgetting to confirm a catering order for a meeting until the absolute last minute, or not replying to a vendor because I opened the email, got distracted by a phone call, and then it just got buried in my inbox. \n\nAnd then with school, it's the same exact thing. I've been missing school deadlines left and right lately. I'll read the syllabus, tell myself a paper is due on Thursday, and then suddenly it's Friday morning and I realize I completely forgot to submit it, or I'll miss those weekly discussion board posts entirely. It's incredibly frustrating. \n\nSo between work and school, it's happening often enough that I really worry it could affect my reputation. I don't want people to think I'm flaky or unreliable, because I'm really not, I just... I just lose track of things. And there’s an Office Manager role opening up next year when our current manager, Susan, retires. Susan has been there for twenty years and she is incredibly organized, like, she literally color-codes her highlighters and has a binder for everything. I really, really want that job. I know I could be good at it if I could just get my brain to cooperate. But if my boss sees me dropping the ball on these little follow-ups, or if I can't even keep my school deadlines straight to finish my coursework, I know it's going to ruin my chance at moving into that Office Manager role. So, yeah, it's definitely impacting me in both of those settings, and it just makes me so anxious that I'm going to sabotage my own future without even meaning to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless, like it’s hard to relax or sit through a long meeting without needing to move around?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I don't get that feeling at all, actually. Sitting still is probably the easiest part of my day! You know, we have these really long staff meetings every other Tuesday—well, sometimes they push them to Wednesday if the VP of Sales is flying in, but usually Tuesday mornings—and I’m the one who has to take all the meeting minutes. So I am literally glued to my chair for sometimes two or two and a half hours straight. And physically, I’m totally fine. I don't feel like I need to jump up or pace around or anything like that. We did just get these new ergonomic mesh chairs in the conference room last month, which are honestly a huge upgrade from the old fake leather ones that used to make your back sweat, so maybe that helps. But even before that, I never really had an issue with needing to move.\n\nIf anything, my body is perfectly happy just staying put. It’s my *brain* that gets up and walks out of the room, you know? Like, I’ll be sitting there, completely still, looking right at whoever is presenting the Q3 projections, and suddenly I realize I haven't heard a single word they said for the last ten minutes because I was mentally reorganizing my kitchen pantry. But physically? No, I'm not restless at all. \n\nAnd it's the exact same thing when it comes to relaxing at home. I have absolutely no problem just melting into my couch. I bought this incredibly soft, chunky knit blanket from Target a few weeks ago—it’s that mustard yellow color that's super trendy right now, it matches my throw pillows perfectly—and once I’m under that with a cup of chamomile tea, I can sit there and binge-watch three or four episodes of whatever true crime documentary I’m into without moving a muscle. I can be a total couch potato. So, yeah, feeling restless or needing to move around really isn't something I struggle with. I can sit still for ages. It's just paying attention while I'm sitting there that's the tricky part!\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find yourself worrying a lot most days, even when there isn’t a clear reason to worry?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not really. I mean, if I'm worrying, there is always a very specific, very real reason for it. Like I was saying earlier, I definitely get anxious about dropping the ball at work or messing up my chances at that Office Manager position when Susan retires. So if I’m lying in bed at night stressing out, it’s usually because my brain suddenly decided to wonder if I actually sent that catering confirmation email, or if I just typed it out and left it sitting in my drafts folder. Which, honestly, happens to me way more than I'd like to admit—I’ll type this perfectly professional email, get distracted by the phone ringing, and then completely forget to hit send. 

But just worrying out of nowhere, for no clear reason? No, I don't really get that. I don't just wake up with a sense of impending doom or anything. My sister, on the other hand, is exactly like that. She will worry that the sky is falling just because it’s cloudy. She called me the other day, actually, right in the middle of my lunch break—I was eating this amazing turkey and cranberry sandwich from the little deli down the street from my office, they make their own cranberry sauce from scratch, it is so good—and she was just completely spiraling because her boss had simply asked her to stop by his office later. She was convinced she was getting fired, and it turned out he just wanted her to sign a birthday card for someone! 

I just don't operate like that. If there’s no fire, I’m not really looking for smoke, you know? My stress is entirely tied to the things I know I’m actively messing up or forgetting, like my school deadlines or my job tasks. If those things are somehow handled—or, let's be real, if I just manage to completely forget about them for a few hours—I’m actually a pretty relaxed person. So, no, I wouldn't say I worry for no reason. If my heart is racing, there's almost always a missed deadline or a lost file behind it!

Like with that phone call I forgot to make, I just sat there at my desk afterward thinking, \"Come on, you're 36 years old, you should be able to remember to make a simple phone call.\" So there are definitely moments where I feel bad about myself, or I get overwhelmed and maybe a little tearful just because it's a lot to manage, but it's not like a deep, dark sadness that lingers all day. \n\nI don't wake up feeling hopeless or anything like that. Because I just don't have those rigid deadlines or people waiting on me at home, so if my brain wanders off, it's just... fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice these attention and forgetfulness problems in more than one setting, like at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying about my apartment and my friends, home is pretty forgiving, so I don't really notice it as a crisis there. But if you mean, is it happening in more than one area where it actually has real consequences... then yes, definitely. Because it's not just my daily job duties, it's my classes, too. \n\nI don't think I mentioned this yet, but I'm actually taking a couple of evening and online classes at the community college—just some business administration and accounting courses, trying to better myself, you know? And I mean, I’m still getting by overall. I haven't failed out, and I haven't been fired. But I’ve missed small work follow-ups, like forgetting to confirm a catering order for a meeting until the absolute last minute, or not replying to a vendor because I opened the email, got distracted by a phone call, and then it just got buried in my inbox. \n\nAnd then with school, it's the same exact thing. I've been missing school deadlines left and right lately. I'll read the syllabus, tell myself a paper is due on Thursday, and then suddenly it's Friday morning and I realize I completely forgot to submit it, or I'll miss those weekly discussion board posts entirely. It's incredibly frustrating. \n\nSo between work and school, it's happening often enough that I really worry it could affect my reputation. I don't want people to think I'm flaky or unreliable, because I'm really not, I just... I just lose track of things. Susan has been there for twenty years and she is incredibly organized, like, she literally color-codes her highlighters and has a binder for everything. I really, really want that job. I know I could be good at it if I could just get my brain to cooperate. But if my boss sees me dropping the ball on these little follow-ups, or if I can't even keep my school deadlines straight to finish my coursework, I know it's going to ruin my chance at moving into that Office Manager role. So, yeah, it's definitely impacting me in both of those settings, and it just makes me so anxious that I'm going to sabotage my own future without even meaning to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless, like it’s hard to relax or sit through a long meeting without needing to move around?\n\nPatient: No, not really. So I am literally glued to my chair for sometimes two or two and a half hours straight. And physically, I’m totally fine. I don't feel like I need to jump up or pace around or anything like that. I can be a total couch potato. So, yeah, feeling restless or needing to move around really isn't something I struggle with. I can sit still for ages. It's just paying attention while I'm sitting there that's the tricky part!\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot most days, even when there isn’t a clear reason to worry?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Which, honestly, happens to me way more than I'd like to admit—I’ll type this perfectly professional email, get distracted by the phone ringing, and then completely forget to hit send. \n\nBut just worrying out of nowhere, for no clear reason?
